=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
An integer sequence of length between 1 and N, inclusive, where each element is between 1 and M, inclusive, is called a good sequence.
The score of a good sequence is defined as the number of positive divisors of X, where X is the product of the elements in the sequence.
There are \displaystyle \sum_{k=1}^{N}M^k good sequences. Find the sum of the scores of all those sequences modulo 998244353.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M

Output

Print the answer as an integer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 10^{18}
- 1 \leq M \leq 16
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

1 7

Sample Output 1

16

There are seven good sequences: (1),(2),(3),(4),(5),(6),(7). Their scores are 1,2,2,3,2,4,2, respectively, so the answer is 1+2+2+3+2+4+2=16.

Sample Input 2

3 11

Sample Output 2

16095

For example, (8,11) and (1,8,2) are good sequences. Here is the process of calculating their scores:

- The product of the elements in (8,11) is 8 \times 11 = 88. 88 has eight positive divisors: 1,2,4,8,11,22,44,88, so the score of (8,11) is 8.
- The product of the elements in (1,8,2) is 1 \times 8 \times 2 = 16. 16 has five positive divisors: 1,2,4,8,16, so the score of (1,8,2) is 5.

Sample Input 3

81131 14

Sample Output 3

182955659

Remember to take the result modulo 998244353.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now, let's examine matrix exponent implementation.

We can optimize by using vector-matrix multiplication instead of full matrix exponent? Since we only need A^{N-1} * v. Implement:
def mat_mul(A,B):
    n=len(A)
    C=[[0]*n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        Ai=A[i]
        Ci=C[i]
        for k in range(n):
            if Ai[k]:
                aik=Ai[k]
                Bk=B[k]
                for j in range(n):
                    Ci[j]=(Ci[j]+aik*Bk[j])%MOD
    return C

Since matrices dense after squaring, no skip maybe. Use local mod. 8 small.

def mat_vec_mul(A,v):
    return [sum(A[i][j]*v[j] for j in range(8))%MOD for i in range(8)]

def mat_pow(A,e):
    n=len(A)
    R=identity
    while e:
       if e&1: R=mat_mul(R,A)
       A=mat_mul(A,A)
       e//=2
    return R

Then v = mat_vec_mul(mat_pow(A,N-1), v) or if N-1=0 identity. Or vector exponent:
def apply_pow(A,e,v):
    while e:
       if e&1: v=mat_vec_mul(A,v)
       A=mat_mul(A,A)
       e//=2
    return v
This works because matrix powers commute? We process bits from LSB: if bit set, apply current A to v. Potential issue: In mat_mul, using % inside inner loop can be slow but 8x8 fine. But we can optimize by accumulating and mod after row. Let's write simple.

```
def mat_mul(A,B):
    n=8
    C=[[0]*n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for k in range(n):
            if A[i][k]:
                aik=A[i][k]
                for j in range(n):
                    C[i][j] = (C[i][j] + aik * B[k][j]) % MOD
    return C
```
Since n small, okay. Could skip if A[i][k]==0; matrices dense after squaring, but okay.

```
def mat_vec_mul(A,v):
    n=8
    res=[0]*n
    for i in range(n):
        s=0
        for j in range(n):
            s += A[i][j]*v[j]
        res[i]=s%MOD
    return res
```

Now, let's plan final code structure:

```
import sys, math

MOD = 998244353
PRIMES = [2,3,5,7,11,13]
P = len(PRIMES)
FULL = (1<<P)-1

def mat_mul(A,B):
    n=8
    C=[[0]*n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        Ai=A[i]
        Ci=C[i]
        for k in range(n):
            aik=Ai[k]
            if aik:
                Bk=B[k]
                for j in range(n):
                    Ci[j] = (Ci[j] + aik * Bk[j]) % MOD
    return C

def mat_vec_mul(A,v):
    n=8
    res=[0]*n
    for i in range(n):
        s=0
        Ai=A[i]
        for j in range(n):
            s += Ai[j]*v[j]
        res[i]=s%MOD
    return res

def solve():
    data=sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data: return
    N=int(data[0]); M=int(data[1])
    # c
    c=[0]*(FULL+1)
    for a in range(1,M+1):
        x=a
        exp=[0]*P
        for idx,p in enumerate(PRIMES):
            while x % p == 0:
                exp[idx]+=1
                x//=p
        for mask in range(1,FULL+1):
            prod=1
            for idx in range(P):
                if mask>>idx &1:
                    prod *= exp[idx]
            c[mask] = (c[mask] + prod) % MOD
    # dp
    dp = [[0]*(FULL+1) for _ in range(P+1)] # P=6, need t up to6
    dp[0][0]=1
    for t in range(1,P+1):
        for mask in range(1,FULL+1):
            lsb = mask & -mask
            total=0
            sub=mask
            while sub:
                if sub & lsb:
                    total = (total + c[sub] * dp[t-1][mask ^ sub]) % MOD
                sub = (sub-1) & mask
            dp[t][mask]=total
    a=[0]*(P+1)
    a[0]=1
    for t in range(1,P+1):
        a[t]=sum(dp[t]) % MOD
    # f1..f7
    f=[0]*(P+2) # indices 1..7? Let's use list length 8 with f[1..7].
    for k in range(1,P+2): # 1..7
        total=0
        for r in range(P+1):
            fall=1
            for i in range(r):
                fall *= (k-i)
            if k >= r:
                total = (total + a[r] * fall * pow(M, k-r, MOD)) % MOD
            # else fall is zero anyway, but skip
        f[k]=total
    # build matrix
    n=8
    A=[[0]*n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n-2): # 0..5
        A[i][i+1]=1
    # row6
    q = M % MOD
    for j in range(7):
        coeff = math.comb(7,j) * pow((-q) % MOD, 7-j, MOD) % MOD
        A[6][j] = (-coeff) % MOD
    # row7
    A[7][1] = 1
    A[7][7] = 1
    v = [f[1], f[2], f[3], f[4], f[5], f[6], f[7], f[1]]
    e = N-1
    while e:
        if e & 1:
            v = mat_vec_mul(A, v)
        e >>= 1
        if e:
            A = mat_mul(A, A)
        # Wait standard squares every iteration even if e becomes 0? If we square only if e after shift, for e=2: e=2 bit0 no apply, e>>=1 ->1, if e: square A^2. Next loop e=1 apply A^2, e>>=1 ->0, if e false no square. Works. For e=3: e=3 bit1 apply A, e>>=1 ->1, if e square A^2. Next loop e=1 apply A^2, e>>=1 ->0. Works. For e=1: bit1 apply A, e>>=1 ->0, no square. Works. This saves one square. But is it correct to square after shifting? We need A = A^2 for next bit. If e after shift nonzero, we square. If e becomes zero, no need. Good.
    print(v[7] % MOD)
```

Wait in the while loop, if we square only if e after shift, for e=4 (binary100):
e=4 bit0 no apply, e>>=1 ->2, e true square A^2.
e=2 bit0 no apply, e>>=1 ->1, e true square A^4.
e=1 bit1 apply A^4, e>>=1 ->0, no square. Good.
So okay. But note if e after shift is nonzero, we square; if e was originally e and we shifted, we need A squared for next bit. This is fine. However, if e after shift is zero, no need. Good.

But standard while e: if e&1: v=mat_vec_mul(A,v); A=mat_mul(A,A); e>>=1. This squares even after last bit. Our version squares after shift only if e remains. Equivalent. Let's ensure for e=1: no square, okay. For e=2: after first iteration no apply, shift e=1, square A^2. Next iteration apply A^2. Good. For e=3: first apply A, shift e=1, square A^2. Next apply A^2. Good. So yes.

But careful: In the loop, if we shift before squaring, the square uses A from current bit, which is correct. But if e after shift is nonzero, we square. If e after shift is zero, no need. Good.

Alternatively use standard to avoid confusion:
```
while e:
    if e & 1:
        v = mat_vec_mul(A, v)
    A = mat_mul(A, A)
    e >>= 1
```

```
def mat_mul(A,B):
    n=8
    C=[[0]*n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            s=0
            for k in range(n):
                s += A[i][k] * B[k][j]
            C[i][j] = s % MOD
    return C
```
This does 8*8*8=512 multiplications and additions, values ~8e18, okay. Matrix entries mod <1e9, product <1e18, sum 8e18, Python int still small. This is simpler. Use this.

Let's draft code carefully:

```
import sys

MOD = 998244353
PRIMES = [2,3,5,7,11,13]
P = len(PRIMES)
FULL = (1 << P) - 1
C7 = [1,7,21,35,35,21,7,1]

def mat_mul(A,B):
    n=8
    C=[[0]*n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        Ai=A[i]
        for k in range(n):
            aik=Ai[k]
            if aik:
                Bk=B[k]
                for j in range(n):
                    C[i][j] = (C[i][j] + aik * Bk[j]) % MOD
    return C
```
This mod inside. Or i,j,k. Which is faster? i,k,j with mod inside maybe okay. But if we use i,j,k with sum after, code simpler:
```
def mat_mul(A,B):
    n=8
    C=[[0]*n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            s=0
            for k in range(n):
                s += A[i][k] * B[k][j]
            C[i][j] = s % MOD
    return C
```
This is 8^3=512, fine. Use this to avoid mod inside inner loop. But note s can be up to 8*(MOD-1)^2 ~8e18, Python int okay. Good.

mat_vec:
```
def mat_vec_mul(A,v):
    n=8
    res=[0]*n
    for i in range(n):
        s=0
        for j in range(n):
            s += A[i][j] * v[j]
        res[i] = s % MOD
    return res
```

solve:
```
def solve():
    data=sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data: return
    N=int(data[0]); M=int(data[1])

    c=[0]*(FULL+1)
    for num in range(1, M+1):
        x=num
        exp=[0]*P
        for idx,p in enumerate(PRIMES):
            while x % p == 0:
                exp[idx]+=1
                x//=p
        for mask in range(1, FULL+1):
            prod=1
            for idx in range(P):
                if (mask >> idx) & 1:
                    prod *= exp[idx]
            c[mask] = (c[mask] + prod) % MOD

    dp = [[0]*(FULL+1) for _ in range(P+1)]
    dp[0][0]=1
    for t in range(1, P+1):
        for mask in range(1, FULL+1):
            lsb = mask & -mask
            total=0
            sub=mask
            while sub:
                if sub & lsb:
                    total = (total + c[sub] * dp[t-1][mask ^ sub]) % MOD
                sub = (sub - 1) & mask
            dp[t][mask] = total

    coef=[0]*(P+1)
    coef[0]=1
    for t in range(1, P+1):
        coef[t] = sum(dp[t]) % MOD

    f=[0]*(P+2)
    for k in range(1, P+2):
        total=0
        for r in range(P+1):
            fall=1
            for i in range(r):
                fall *= (k - i)
            if k >= r:
                total = (total + coef[r] * fall * pow(M, k-r, MOD)) % MOD
        f[k]=total

    A=[[0]*8 for _ in range(8)]
    for i in range(6):
        A[i][i+1]=1
    q = M % MOD
    for j in range(7):
        coeff = C7[j] * pow((-q) % MOD, 7-j, MOD) % MOD
        A[6][j] = (-coeff) % MOD
    A[7][1] = 1
    A[7][7] = 1

    v=[f[1], f[2], f[3], f[4], f[5], f[6], f[7], f[1]]
    e=N-1
    while e:
        if e & 1:
            v = mat_vec_mul(A, v)
        A = mat_mul(A, A)
        e >>= 1
    print(v[7] % MOD)
```

Let's think about if in vector exponent, squaring A after applying bit but before shifting is standard. For e=2: e=2 bit0 no apply, A=A^2, e=1; bit1 apply A^2. Good. For e=3: apply A, A=A^2, e=1; apply A^2. Good. For e=4: e=4 bit0 no apply, A=A^2, e=2; bit0 no apply, A=A^4, e=1; apply A^4. Good. Fine.

Now, let's think about if `mat_mul(A,A)` after e becomes 0? Standard squares even after last bit, but if e=0 loop ends. For e=1, it squares A unnecessarily then shifts to0. This is okay but maybe if e=1 and A is large, one extra square. Fine. Could optimize:
```
while e:
    if e & 1: v=...
    e >>= 1
    if e:
        A=mat_mul(A,A)
```
But standard simpler. Use standard.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with `while x % p == 0` when x=1 and p=2: 1%2=1, okay. For p=2, if x becomes1, no infinite. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `exp` for num=1: all zeros. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with `c[mask]` for mask where prod=0; adding0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `dp[t-1][mask ^ sub]` when mask^sub has lsb smaller than sub? Doesn't matter; dp already computed for all masks for t-1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with `sum(dp[t])` includes dp[t][0] which is 0 for t>0; okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `coef[r] * fall * pow(M, k-r, MOD)` maybe large but Python int okay. We mod after addition. fall up to5040, coef<MOD, pow<MOD, product ~5e3*1e9*1e9=5e21? Wait 5040*1e9*1e9=5e21, Python int okay but bigger. Actually coef<1e9, pow<1e9, product ~5e21, still small. total sum 7 terms ~3e22, okay. Could mod after each addition. We do `% MOD` after addition. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `pow(M, k-r, MOD)` for k-r maybe 0 and M maybe 0? no.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "M" maybe 16 and q=16, `(-q)%MOD` = MOD-16. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "C7" coefficients and recurrence if q=1: We computed. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "A[6][j] = (-coeff) % MOD" where coeff = C7[j] * (-q)^{7-j}. This equals - C7[j] (-q)^{7-j}. Yes.

Let's maybe derive row6 coefficients from code:
`coeff = C7[j] * pow((-q) % MOD, 7-j, MOD) % MOD`
This is C(7,j)(-q)^{7-j}.
`A[6][j] = (-coeff) % MOD` = -C(7,j)(-q)^{7-j}. This matches c[j]. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with row6 if q=1 and j=0: coeff=1*(-1)^7 = MOD-1. A=-coeff=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "f" list if P+2=8, range(1,8) gives k=1..7. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "v" list if f[1] computed; yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "N" as int and "e=N-1" if N=0? no.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "MOD" in mat_mul if using i,j,k and s maybe 8e18; okay. But if we use vector exponent, A is squared many times; entries mod. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "mat_mul" using `for k in range(n)` inside `for j`; maybe B[k][j] access pattern not cache-friendly but n small. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "mat_vec_mul" if v entries mod; yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "print(v[7] % MOD)" v[7] already mod but okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "coef" maybe a_r for r=0 should be 1 and f_k includes M^k. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "c" for mask maybe should include a=1? no.

Let's prove by induction: For any partition of mask into t blocks, let B be block containing lsb(mask). Then B is a nonempty subset of mask containing lsb. Removing B leaves a partition of mask\B into t-1 blocks. The recurrence sums over all B containing lsb, c[B] * dp[t-1][mask\B]. Since dp[t-1] counts partitions of remaining. Each partition counted once. Good.

Each partition counted once. Good.

Each partition counted once. Good.

Removing B leaves a partition of mask\B into t-1 blocks. The recurrence sums over all B containing lsb, c[B] * dp[t-1][mask\B]. Since dp[t-1] counts partitions of remaining. Each partition counted once. Good.

Let's prove by induction: For any partition of mask into t blocks, let B be block containing lsb(mask). Then B is a nonempty subset of mask containing lsb. Removing B leaves a partition of mask\B into t-1 blocks. The recurrence sums over all B containing lsb, c[B] * dp[t-1][mask\B]. Since dp[t-1] counts partitions of remaining. Each partition counted once. Good.

Each partition counted once. Good.

Each partition counted once. Good.

Removing B leaves a partition of mask\B into t-1 blocks. The recurrence sums over all B containing lsb, c[B] * dp[t-1][mask\B]. Since dp[t-1] counts partitions of remaining. Each partition counted once. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353
PRIMES = [2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13]
P = len(PRIMES)
FULL = (1 << P) - 1
C7 = [1, 7, 21, 35, 35, 21, 7, 1]


def mat_mul(A, B):
    n = 8
    C = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            s = 0
            for k in range(n):
                s += A[i][k] * B[k][j]
            C[i][j] = s % MOD
    return C


def mat_vec_mul(A, v):
    n = 8
    res = [0] * n
    for i in range(n):
        s = 0
        for j in range(n):
            s += A[i][j] * v[j]
        res[i] = s % MOD
    return res


def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    N = int(data[0])
    M = int(data[1])

    # c[mask] = sum over elements a of product_{p in mask} v_p(a)
    c = [0] * (FULL + 1)
    for x0 in range(1, M + 1):
        x = x0
        exp = [0] * P
        for i, p in enumerate(PRIMES):
            while x % p == 0:
                exp[i] += 1
                x //= p

        for mask in range(1, FULL + 1):
            prod = 1
            for i in range(P):
                if (mask >> i) & 1:
                    prod *= exp[i]
            c[mask] = (c[mask] + prod) % MOD

    # dp[t][mask] = sum of products of c[block] over partitions of mask into t blocks
    dp = [[0] * (FULL + 1) for _ in range(P + 1)]
    dp[0][0] = 1

    for t in range(1, P + 1):
        for mask in range(1, FULL + 1):
            lsb = mask & -mask
            total = 0
            sub = mask
            while sub:
                if sub & lsb:
                    total = (total + c[sub] * dp[t - 1][mask ^ sub]) % MOD
                sub = (sub - 1) & mask
            dp[t][mask] = total

    # a[r] is the coefficient of (k)_r * M^(k-r) in f_k
    a = [0] * (P + 1)
    a[0] = 1
    for t in range(1, P + 1):
        a[t] = sum(dp[t]) % MOD

    # f_k = sum of scores over all sequences of length exactly k
    f = [0] * (P + 2)  # f[1]..f[7]
    for k in range(1, P + 2):
        total = 0
        for r in range(P + 1):
            fall = 1
            for i in range(r):
                fall *= (k - i)
            if k >= r:
                total = (total + a[r] * fall * pow(M, k - r, MOD)) % MOD
        f[k] = total

    # Build transition matrix for state:
    # [f_k, f_{k+1}, ..., f_{k+6}, S_k]^T
    A = [[0] * 8 for _ in range(8)]

    for i in range(6):
        A[i][i + 1] = 1

    q = M % MOD
    # (E - q)^7 annihilates f_k
    for j in range(7):
        coeff = C7[j] * pow((-q) % MOD, 7 - j, MOD) % MOD
        A[6][j] = (-coeff) % MOD

    # S_{k+1} = S_k + f_{k+1}
    A[7][1] = 1
    A[7][7] = 1

    v = [f[1], f[2], f[3], f[4], f[5], f[6], f[7], f[1]]

    e = N - 1
    while e:
        if e & 1:
            v = mat_vec_mul(A, v)
        A = mat_mul(A, A)
        e >>= 1

    print(v[7] % MOD)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
